A `past` refusal for something that is not moving in time was wrong on every caller, not only this one, so the fix belongs in the rule rather than in an exemption each caller has to remember.

    **`resize-test` CAUGHT THE FIRST VERSION OF `datesMoved`**, and the catch is the useful part: it compared `start`/`end`/`startHour`, so a RESIZE — which does not move the start and may not change the day — read as standing still, and an op grown across its own assignee's day off stopped being refused. `endHour` and `hpd` count too. A rule about "when the work sits" has to include how much of the day it takes.

    TESTED red-first, `scripts/assign-refusal-test.mjs`, 25 assertions, wired (85 suites). 16 mutants, 16 caught — after one SKIPPED on `if (refusal) { showLandingRefusal(...` , which is identical in `commitAssign` and `commitDates`. R4 in the mutants again.

    **AND ONE OF THIS SUITE'S OWN ASSERTIONS WAS GREEN ON THE UNFIXED CODE.** "refuseLanding comes before commitLanding" was written as `body.indexOf(a) < body.indexOf(b)`, and `indexOf` returns **-1** when absent — so "A comes before B" is true whenever **A is missing**, which is precisely the state the suite exists to catch. Every ordering assertion now requires both ends to be found. That is R4's shape on a third axis: not "is the string somewhere else" but "does absence satisfy the comparison".

426. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT BUILT] **THE DEPARTMENT PRECEDENCE HAZARD, which department-on-drop will be the first thing to create.** `unitDepartments` walks op → panel → job and the NEAREST level that states anything wins outright — no union. Today nothing exercises it (#425: 23 ops state their own, zero inherit). The approved rewrite writes an own department onto the OP and leaves its parents alone, which is right for the gesture and is also how a panel and its ops begin to disagree. FIXED 2026-10-07, and **the fix is the picker, not the precedence** — precedence is right, the nearest statement wins, and that is exactly what makes an override an override. **MY OWN FRAMING OF THE DEFECT WAS BACKWARDS AND THE MEASUREMENT CORRECTED IT.** The note said the picker "keeps showing the panel's value as though it applied". It does not: every picker called `unitDepartments(node, **null, null**)`, so an inheriting op resolved to NOTHING and the control drew it as unconstrained. **TWO SURFACES, TWO DIFFERENT WRONG ANSWERS FOR ONE STATE, which is the two-surfaces shape again on a control nobody has ever used.** The edit-job form renders `MultiDrop`, whose `emptyLabel` defaults to **"Anyone"** — an outright false statement about an op its panel constrains, and the scheduler enforces that constraint while the screen denies it. The schedule-modal pickers render `node.requiredDepartment || "Dept"`, the dimmed placeholder, which reads as *unset*. Neither is true, they disagree with each other, and they disagree in a way where the more confident one is the more wrong. The fix is `resolveDepartments(node, panel, job)` returning `{ depts, from: "own" | "panel" | "job" | "none" }`, added BESIDE `unitDepartments` — which keeps its signature and all eight callers including the server path, but now delegates, because two copies of a precedence rule is precisely how this field ended up with four disagreeing readers (#289). Both surfaces state the origin: an inherited set reads `"Admin · from operation"` in italic secondary text, an own set reads `"Admin"` in accent. **The UI's nouns, not the code's** — a panel is an "operation" and an op is a "task" everywhere the user can see, so "from operation" is what a task inheriting from its panel says. `departmentsAfterToggle` starts from the EFFECTIVE set rather than the node's own, because the control now shows what applies and a click has to edit what you can see; starting from an inheriting node's empty own-set would make the first click silently DROP the inherited department instead of adding to it. CLEAR-TO-INHERIT is an explicit row (`Use the operation's department (Admin)`), offered only when the node is overriding something that exists — on a node with nothing above it would be indistinguishable from "set to anyone", which is a different statement and the one that cannot be made (#439). MEASURED before building (`scripts/measure-dept-inherit.mjs`): **0 ops inherit** — 0 from a panel, 0 from a job — so this is wholly unexercised machinery; 23 ops state their own, 89 are genuinely unconstrained, 2 panels state one, and the org has 11 configured roles. WHAT THE FIRST PERSON WOULD HAVE SEEN, and it is one click away: the panel `TRAQS Development Pt. 2 / Developement` says Admin over three ops that each also say Admin. Untick Admin on one and the picker would have gone to **"Anyone"** while `unitDepartments` still resolved it to **Admin** and the scheduler still refused everyone outside it — the screen and the rule disagreeing with nothing to look at. `scripts/dept-inherit-test.mjs`, 51 assertions, 24 mutants caught and 0 survived. SIX MUTANTS SURVIVED THE FIRST RUN, ALL MINE, ALL THE SAME MISTAKE AS #414's ONE TURN EARLIER: deleting `inheritedFrom=` from the call site left `/inheritedFrom/` green, because the prop is still named in the signature. Checking that a name APPEARS is not checking that it is WIRED, and a 31,000-line file will almost always contain the name somewhere. Four assertions in `replan-ui-test` were re-anchored rather than rewritten: they pinned `unitDepartments(op, null, null)` and `toggleDept(panel,r)`, which were the defective spellings themselves, so the rule is unchanged and only its anchor moved (R3). A fifth there sliced `MultiDrop` to a fixed 3,600 characters and went red because this pass made the function longer — **a magic width is a slice that expires**, and it now slices to the next function. ONE MORE THING WAS CAUGHT BY READING MY OWN DIFF RATHER THAN BY A TEST, and it is the entry's own subject reappearing inside the fix for it: the clear row and the limitation line were built into `MultiDrop` only, so the EDIT FORM explained what it could not do while the two schedule-modal pickers — where the same gesture is available — said nothing. That is the two-surfaces problem recreated one level down. Both are now `DeptClearRow` and `DeptInheritNote`, rendered by all three pickers, with `deptInheritable` and `deptParentWord` shared beneath them; the wording IS the deliverable for #439, so it has exactly one definition. `MultiDrop` briefly carried its own copy of the operation/job noun mapping beside `deptParentWord`, which is asserted against now — `(RAW.match(/\? "job" : "operation"/g) || []).length === 1`.

    The trap is quiet because the precedence rule RESOLVES the disagreement rather than reporting it: once an op states a department, its panel's value stops meaning anything for that op, permanently and with nothing on screen to say so. Set a panel to "Wire" expecting its ops to follow, and the ones a drag has touched will not — and the panel picker will keep showing "Wire" as though it applied.

    Costs nothing today (two panels state a department: one has no live ops, the other's three ops already restate it). Bites the first time someone uses the panel-level picker after #427 ships.

    **THE FIX IS PROBABLY THE PICKER, NOT THE PRECEDENCE.** Precedence is right: the nearest statement wins, which is what makes an override an override. What is missing is that the picker shows a value without saying whether it still APPLIES — it renders the panel's set with no indication that three of its ops have overridden it. Showing inherited-vs-overridden (a count, a muted "2 ops override this", anything) turns a silent resolution into a visible one, and leaves the rule alone. Changing precedence instead would make an op's own department stop meaning what it says, which is a worse trade. NOT BUILT — logged so the decision is made deliberately rather than discovered.

427. **FIXED 2026-10-07 — DEPARTMENT FOLLOWS THE WORK. Reverses root cause 7 chunk B.** A drop that put someone outside the unit's required department on it was REFUSED and the department named. It now SUCCEEDS and the department becomes where the work went: if it moves to someone in Cut, it is Cut work.

    **THE MEASUREMENT DECIDED TWO OF THE THREE RULINGS BY MAKING THEM MOOT.** Of Matrix's 112 live ops, 23 state their own department, **ZERO inherit**, and **ZERO name more than one** (#425). So "union or replace" and "the op or its parent" are both questions about machinery no live data exercises, and the rulings made on them will be the first things that ever do.

    **REPLACE, NOT UNION.** Trey: *"union produces a value nobody can read as intent"* — "Wire or Cut or Layout" is indistinguishable from someone having ticked three boxes. The new set is the added people's departments. What it loses is real and worth stating: if an op genuinely was "Wire or Cut", a drop onto Cut discards "Wire" and nothing records that the op was ever flexible — except the moveLog, which is the point below.

    **THE REPLACED SET GOES IN THE moveLog, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE DIFFERENCE FROM #341.** `fromDepartments`/`toDepartments` sit beside the `fromTeam`/`toTeam` that caused the change. #341 wrote a guess into the data and left 87 ops carrying a constraint nobody typed and **nowhere to look afterwards**; this writes a constraint from a gesture and says what it replaced. The keys are ABSENT when nothing changed, so the log does not fill with entries that read like a change.

    **ON THE REFUSAL PATH ONLY — the ruling the measurement forced.** A drop onto an op that said "anyone" writes NOTHING. **89 of 112 ops (79%) are unconstrained**, so a rewrite on every drop would take the board from 79% free to 0% one drag at a time, which is #341 exactly. Narrowing a stated department in the direction the work went is EDITING A FACT; creating one on an unconstrained op is INVENTING A CONSTRAINT FROM A GESTURE. Different operations wearing the same gesture, and only one persists.

    **THE OP ONLY.** Its panel and job keep what they said. Right for the gesture, and also how a panel and its ops begin to disagree — logged as #426, where the conclusion is that the PICKER should show inherited-vs-overridden rather than the precedence changing.

    ONE EDGE WORTH KNOWING: dropping on somebody with **no** department widens the op to "anyone". It is the only case that removes a constraint rather than narrowing, it is coherent (the work went to someone unrestricted), and it is unreachable at Matrix, where all 18 people hold exactly one department.

    **BUILT ON #424, which was the prerequisite.** Without it the rule would have landed on the drag paths only and `commitAssign` would have accepted the drop AND left the department stale — a third behaviour, worse than the two it replaced. `departmentFollow` is applied in `applyDragMove`, so every path that writes a move gets it; all six call sites are asserted to pass `people`, because a caller that forgets it recreates exactly that third behaviour silently.

    THE SERVER ACCEPTS IT, CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED: `scheduleRules`' department rule reads the INCOMING node (`unitDepartments(n, after.panel, after.job)`), so the new set and the new team arrive together and agree. A rule comparing against the stored department would have rejected every rewrite and the feature would have failed on write with nothing to see.

    TESTED red-first, `scripts/department-follow-test.mjs`, 37 assertions, wired (86 suites). **16 mutants, 16 caught** — after five survived and two were skipped, and what they exposed is below.

    **TWO GUARDS WERE DEAD CODE AND MUTATION PROVED IT.** `if (depts.length === 0) return null` and `if (key(to) === key(depts)) return null` could not be told from their own absence: `personDeptMatch` already returns true for every person when the requirement is empty, so nobody is ever "outside" an unconstrained unit; and `outside` is by construction the people with NO overlap with `depts`, so the new set is always disjoint from the old. Two mechanisms for one decision is a place for them to drift (#70's second clamp), so the condition is stated once.

    **AND TWO OF THIS SUITE'S FIXTURES PROVED NOTHING.** "A plain date move leaves the department alone" used the SAME team on both sides, which the added-people filter already empties — so removing the `reassigned` guard changed no assertion. It carries different teams now, which is the real shape: `applyDragMove` only writes `team` when `reassigned`, so a mover with a different `to.team` and no flag must not rewrite the department either. The second was missing entirely: an op already out of department, gaining an in-department person, must not be rewritten on the strength of the member who was already there.

    FIVE EXISTING ASSERTIONS REWRITTEN, NOT DELETED (R3): `day-drag-test`'s whole department section pinned the refusal. It now pins the follow — same conditions, opposite response — including that it happens on Basic too, which this never was a paid rule. `assign-cell-test`'s moveLog literal gained `people`, and two assertions in `assign-refusal-test` from #424 were rewritten the same way.

428. **FIXED 2026-10-07 — DELETING A COLUMN ON THE JOBS LIST CAME BACK ON RELOAD, AND THE CAUSE IS THAT "HIDDEN" AND "NEWLY SHIPPED" ARE THE SAME STATE.** Reported by Trey.

    Hiding a standard column removes its id from `colOrder` (`:27911`). On load, both readers run it through:

        const backfillColOrder = (saved) => {
          const known = STD_COL_DEFS.map(c => c.id);
          const kept = saved.filter(id => known.includes(id));
          return [...kept, ...known.filter(id => !kept.includes(id))];   // <- adds back everything missing
        };

    **ABSENCE FROM THE ARRAY MEANS TWO DIFFERENT THINGS — "I hid this" and "this column shipped after you last saved" — and backfill resolves the ambiguity in favour of the second, every time.** Its own comment says why it exists: *"without it a column shipped after this account last saved its order is stripped a moment after appearing, on every single load."* That problem is real; the encoding is what makes the two indistinguishable.

    TWO FACTS, ONE ENCODING — the same shape `scheduleRules` records for departments: *"'Wire or Cut can do this' had to be written as 'nothing', which is also how you write 'anyone'."* It was solved there by storing the SET explicitly and making empty canonical. The same move works here: store which columns are HIDDEN, rather than inferring it from which are missing from an order list.

    It fails on a plain refresh, not only across browsers: backfill runs in the localStorage initialiser (`:5806`) AND in the remote load (`:8465`), and the remote read is the one that wins because it lands after first paint.

429. **RULING 2026-10-07 — THE VALUE IS SHARED, THE VISIBILITY IS PERSONAL.** Trey: *"The contents of a column (PO numbers, whatever) are shared and sync to everyone when someone sets them. Whether a given person shows or hides that column is theirs alone, and must survive a reload and ideally a browser change. Same for any show/hide of a cell or field."*

    **WHAT EXISTS TODAY — FOUR STORES, USED INCONSISTENTLY:**

        orgSettings          S3, shared, admin-gated   column DEFINITIONS (customCols), status/
                                                       priority lists, the shop calendar
        user-settings blob   S3, PER ACCOUNT           themeMode, customTheme, colOrder, colLabels,
                             survives a browser change groupColPref, userPrefs  — six things, total
        usePersistedUI       localStorage, per browser 26 pieces of view state
        plain useState       nothing at all            colWidths, tMode, taskSubView,
                                                       scheduleTeamMode, tsAdminTab

    **THE ACCOUNT-LEVEL STORE ALREADY EXISTS AND ALREADY CLAIMS THIS JOB.** `netlify/functions/user-settings.js` is keyed by the authenticated email server-side, so a user can only read or write their own, and its header says it holds *"job-list column layout"*. Column order, labels and grouping are in it. The mechanism is not missing — it is under-used.

    **THE ANSWER TO "WHAT ELSE IS AT ORG LEVEL THAT IS REALLY PERSONAL" IS: ESSENTIALLY NOTHING, AND THE MISPLACEMENT RUNS THE OTHER WAY.** Every key in `orgSettings` was checked. `workDays`, `holidays`, `lunch`, `breaks`, `workStart`/`workEnd`, `timeZone`, `payDates`, `payPeriodHourCap`, `trackLunch`/`trackBreaks` are the shop's calendar and payroll; `roles`, `signOffTemplates`, `approvalSteps`, `conditions`, `approverLabel`, `approvalQueueLabel`, `orgLogo`, `iosPayClockEnabled` are org policy; `statusOpts`/`priOpts` are org DELIBERATELY and the reason is recorded in place (an admin's rename must be visible to everyone); `customCols` is the column's definition, which is shared by the same logic as its contents. **One judgement call: `exportTemplates`** — saved export layouts, org-wide. Shared presets are defensible for a team, so it is a question rather than a defect.

    **THE REAL GAP IS PERSONAL PREFERENCES STORED NOWHERE OR LOCALLY.** Against the ruling's bar — survive a reload, ideally a browser change:

        colWidths            NOWHERE. Resizing a column is lost on every refresh.
        tMode, taskSubView,  NOWHERE. Which schedule view, which Jobs sub-view,
        scheduleTeamMode,    one-row-vs-team, which Time Clock tab — all reset.
        tsAdminTab
        26 x usePersistedUI  localStorage only. Survives a reload, lost on a new
                             browser or machine — the ruling's second half.

    So the work is not moving things OUT of org settings. It is deciding which of these belong in the per-account blob that already exists, and fixing the encoding that makes hiding unrepresentable (#428).

430. **FIXED 2026-10-07 — ONE BUTTON LABELLED "Delete Column" DID TWO DIFFERENT THINGS, AND NOTHING ON SCREEN SAYS WHICH.**

        custom column    removeCustomCol(colId)  -> drops it from orgSettings.customCols,
                                                    FOR THE WHOLE ORGANISATION, with its data
        standard column  colOrder.filter(...)    -> hides it FOR THIS PERSON (and fails to
                                                    persist at all — #428)

    Same context menu, same label, same icon. One is destructive and shared; the other is a personal view toggle. Under #429's ruling they are not even the same KIND of operation — one changes the value, the other changes the visibility — so a single verb cannot be right for both.

    R3's family seen from the other side: not a label that outlived its behaviour, but **one label covering two behaviours that were never the same**. The fix is a naming and UI question ("Hide column" for the personal one, "Delete column" with a confirm for the shared one), and it should be settled in the same pass as #428 because the same menu row is the thing being changed.

    ─── WHAT WAS BUILT (#428 / #429 items 1 and 3 / #430) ───

    **THE ENCODING, which is the whole fix.** `src/columnPrefs.js` splits the two facts that shared one slot:

        colOrder    ORDER ONLY, always complete. Backfill still appends a newly
                    shipped column, which is all it was ever for.
        hiddenCols  WHICH ARE HIDDEN. Explicit, so absence from the order no
                    longer carries a second meaning.

    Both live in the per-account blob, so the preference follows the person to another browser — the second half of #429's bar. The known-column list is passed IN rather than copied into the module: it belongs with the grid that defines it, and a second copy is a second thing to drift.

    **THE MIGRATION IS ONE BOUNDED GUESS, MADE ONCE, AND IT IS STATED RATHER THAN BURIED.** A blob saved before this carries its hidden columns only as gaps in `colOrder` — the last moment the old encoding can be read. `hiddenFromLegacy` reads it and writes the answer down explicitly. **The trade: a column that shipped AFTER that account last saved is indistinguishable from one they hid, and migration reads it as hidden.** That is the same ambiguity that caused the defect, met one final time. What makes it a different thing from #341 is that it happens once, it is visible (the picker lists hidden columns), and it is undone in a click — #341 wrote its guess into SHARED data with nowhere to look afterwards.

    **#429 item 3: `colWidths` now persists.** It was plain `useState` with hard-coded defaults, so resizing a column was lost on every refresh — the one preference in Trey's complaint's family that survived nothing at all. The existing effect that pads or trims it against `customCols.length` still runs, so a blob saved against a different custom-column count self-heals rather than desyncing the grid from its headers.

    **#430: the verb is split, and the destructive one asks.** "Hide Column" for a standard column, "Delete Column" for a custom one, behind a confirm that says what it actually does: *"This removes it and its data for everyone in the organization, not just you."* Trey's reason for doing it in the same pass rather than later: *"one person clicking it on the wrong column loses org data."* The old label gave no hint which of the two you were about to get.

    TESTED red-first, `scripts/col-visibility-test.mjs`, 46 assertions, wired (87 suites). **20 mutants, 20 caught.**

    **THREE SURVIVED THE FIRST RUN AND ALL THREE WERE R4 — IN THE SUITE WRITTEN THE DAY R4 WAS ADDED.** `visibleColOrder(colOrder, hiddenCols)` appears TWICE (the grid and the grouping dropdown), so reverting the grid's call left a bare presence check green. `/remote\.hiddenCols/` also matches the `if` that guards the call, so replacing the call with `setHiddenCols([])` changed nothing the suite could see. And asserting that the confirm block merely CONTAINS `removeCustomCol` let a mutant call it outright and return before opening the dialog — the dead confirm below still satisfied the pattern. Each is now named by site, by call, or by count.

    FOUR EXISTING ASSERTIONS UPDATED, NOT DELETED (R3): `assignee-col-test` pinned the old encoding in four places. One of them SLICED the function body out of TRAQS.jsx with a regex and re-executed it, which broke the moment the body became an expression — it imports the real function from `columnPrefs.js` now, so there is no copy to drift and no slice to re-point.

431. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT BUILT] **THE 26 `usePersistedUI` PREFERENCES ARE LOCALSTORAGE-ONLY, AND MOVING THEM IS A MIGRATION WITH A REAL DECISION IN IT.** `usePersistedUI(name, fallback)` keys on `tq_ui_{orgCode}_{name}` in localStorage: it survives a reload and is lost on a new browser or machine, which meets half of #429's bar. Twenty-six pieces of view state use it. **PARTLY BUILT 2026-10-07: SEVEN MOVE, NINETEEN STAY, AND THE WRITE WAS FIXED FIRST (#441).** THE ORDER MATTERED MORE THAN THE MIGRATION. Measuring the blocker produced an argument against the whole job as originally framed: the blob was replaced wholesale and read once per session, so every key added to it is another TRIGGER for a stale-tab write that reverts the keys already there — and the keys already there are the expensive ones (`colOrder`, `colLabels`, `colWidths`, `hiddenCols`, `customTheme`: minutes of dragging and renaming, against a theme toggle that is one click). Migrating 26 low-value preferences would not have introduced the problem, it would have **made the existing one more likely to fire**. So #441 went first and nothing migrated until it was done. **WHAT MOVES — the seven you set on purpose and would expect to find again:** `grouping`, `jobSort`, `colSort`, `gSort`, `jobsView`, `showCompleted`, `adminFilter`. Each keeps its `usePersistedUI` hook, which paints the first frame, and gains a type-checked read from the remote blob that lands after it and wins — the arrangement `colOrder` already had. **WHAT STAYS, AND WHY, because the next person will see nineteen localStorage keys and read the inconsistency as the defect.** (1) FREE TEXT IS WORSE THAN INCONSISTENT: `fJobNum`, `sFJobNum` and `fCustom` hold a string somebody typed, and carried to another machine they filter a board for a reason the user cannot see and does not remember setting. (2) A FILTER IS A CURRENT INVESTIGATION, NOT A PREFERENCE: the eleven `f*`/`sF*` filters HIDE WORK. Left on, they hide it on one machine today; migrated, they would hide it everywhere, turning a personal annoyance into a support call about missing jobs. (3) TRANSIENT STATE HAS NO MEANING ELSEWHERE: `expandedJobs`, `groupCollapsed` and `tCollapsed` are id sets that grow with the board, and `pmSectionsCollapsed`/`detailSecClosed` are which accordions you left open. That is 6 + 11 (counting `fOverloaded` and `fDeptEqTitle`, both of which `activeFilterCount` counts as filters) + 5 = nineteen. **THE WRITE-COUNT QUESTION, MEASURED AFTER rather than assumed** (`scripts/measure-user-prefs.mjs` section 4): merging does NOT make the endpoint chattier — the 900ms debounce and the no-change skip are untouched, so one settled change is still one write, and a session that lays out the Jobs list is 3 writes before and 3 after while the payload drops from 606 B to 387 B. **The migration does add writes, and that is the honest cost:** a session spent sorting and switching views was 0 writes and is now 4, because those preferences used to touch localStorage only. A full day went 3 → 7. ONE COUNTER-INTUITIVE RESULT WORTH RECORDING: for a single-change session the patch is LARGER than the old wholesale write (342 B vs 202 B), because the baseline starts null so the first write of every session carries everything, and the bundle is now fifteen keys rather than eight. The saving is real but it is a per-session saving, not a per-write one.

    NOT MOVED WITH #428, on Trey's ruling: *"what happens when two browsers disagree"* is a question the column fix does not have to answer — `hiddenCols` has one writer at a time in practice — and twenty-six at once would answer it by accident. The per-account blob is replaced wholesale on write, so two tabs or two machines with different view state would overwrite each other last-write-wins, silently. That is tolerable for a theme and not obviously tolerable for everything.

    Its own pass, with the conflict rule decided first.

432. [RECORDED 2026-10-07] **THE MISPLACEMENT RAN THE OPPOSITE WAY TO THE ONE WE WENT LOOKING FOR, AND THAT IS WORTH HAVING WRITTEN DOWN.** The question asked was: *"what else is stored at org level that is really a personal view preference? If one view preference is in the wrong place, others probably are."* It is a good instinct and the measurement said no.

    **Every key in `orgSettings` was checked and NONE is really personal.** `workDays`, `holidays`, `lunch`, `breaks`, `workStart`/`workEnd`, `timeZone`, `payDates`, `payPeriodHourCap`, `trackLunch`/`trackBreaks` are the shop's calendar and payroll. `roles`, `signOffTemplates`, `approvalSteps`, `conditions`, `approverLabel`, `approvalQueueLabel`, `orgLogo`, `iosPayClockEnabled` are org policy. `statusOpts`/`priOpts` are org DELIBERATELY, with the reason recorded where they live — held per-account, an admin's rename was invisible to everyone else. `customCols` is a column's DEFINITION, shared by exactly the logic #429 states about its contents. `exportTemplates` is the one judgement call and Trey ruled it stays: shared presets are right for a team and nobody has complained.

    **THE GAP IS THE OTHER DIRECTION: personal preferences stored NOWHERE or only locally.** `colWidths` and four view toggles (`tMode`, `taskSubView`, `scheduleTeamMode`, `tsAdminTab`) persisted nothing at all; 26 more are localStorage-only (#431). So the correction to the instinct is: the org/personal boundary is sound, and what is missing is the personal side having somewhere to live. Recorded because the shape of the answer is the reverse of the shape of the question, and the next person to ask it should start from here rather than re-auditing `orgSettings`.

433. [TRIED AND REVERTED 2026-10-07] **THE DROP FADE WAS TOO MUCH.** The schedule bar arrived from 6px above with a brightness lift; Trey asked for a plain fade in place. Built as `filter: opacity()` rather than the `opacity` property, deliberately — #117 records that animating the PROPERTY meant the animation owned it for its whole run, and opacity is where the hover dim, the finished fade and the drag ghosting all live, so a just-dropped bar ignored all three for 250ms.

    **REVERTED ON SIGHT: a bar going to fully transparent and back reads as HEAVIER than the arrival it replaced, not lighter.** Logged rather than deleted because the next person to dislike the drop animation will reach for the same fix, and the useful part is that it was tried, it works technically, and it looks worse. The `filter:` channel trick stands if a fade is ever wanted somewhere else.

    One thing the attempt left behind, worth knowing before anyone revisits it: **#117's guard matches the WORD `opacity` anywhere in the keyframe**, so `filter: opacity()` tripped it while the property stayed free. That is a real imprecision, and it is back as it was. Tighten it to the declaration first.

434. **FIXED 2026-10-07 — A REFUSED DROP PLAYED THE LANDING ANIMATION.** Found while reviewing #433, predates it, and is independent of which animation is used.

    `setDroppedBarId` was the FIRST statement in the schedule's mouseup handler, and **six early returns sit between it and the commit**:

        setDroppedBarId(_dropId)              <- fired immediately
        ...
        return;                          x2   no-op / missing reassign permission
        showDepSiblingError(...); return;     dependency sibling
        if (_refusal) { ... return; }         overlap, time off, the past, department
        _refused({ splitPermission }); return;
        setTasks(prev => _build(prev));       <- the bar actually moves HERE

    So a drop refused for any of those reasons still animated a bar that never moved, while the refusal dialog opened over it. **It does not even need a remount:** React changes the `animation` style from `undefined` to `barDropIn`, which starts it on the element already there.

    **IT MATTERS MORE NOW THAN IT DID.** #424 put the department and time-off rules on the assign path, and #427 turned a cross-department drop into a rewrite rather than a refusal — so the refusals that remain are the ones a user hits by accident, and every one of them was being congratulated with a landing animation.

    The flag now sits with `setTasks`. TESTED red-first, `scripts/drop-flash-order-test.mjs`, 15 assertions, wired (88 suites). **THE TEST PINS THE ORDER, NOT THE LINE:** it locates the flag and asserts that NO `return;` survives after it, so the flag may move anywhere the commit is and may not drift back above a refusal. A count guard on the number of early returns rides along — if the handler is restructured, the assertions are reasoning about something else and should be re-read rather than quietly still passing.

    8 mutants, 8 caught, including the two that put the flag back at the top of mouseup and just above the last refusal. Three were SKIPPED on a first run because the anchors were written with the wrong indentation — re-anchored rather than left, since a skipped mutant proves nothing and these three were the defect itself.

435. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT FIXED] **`barDropIn` IGNORES `prefers-reduced-motion`.** The reduced-motion block disables `.msg-in-mine`, `.msg-in-other`, `.msg-read-pop`, `.msg-time-in`, `.msg-time-out` and `.tq-liquid-blob`, and shortens `.rv-hero`/`.rv-hgrid`. The schedule drop animation is in none of them, so a bar still travels 6px and lifts in brightness for someone who has asked the OS not to animate things.

    A 250ms nudge is mild, which is presumably why nobody has noticed — but **the inconsistency is the finding, not the severity**: this file has a reduced-motion policy and one animation sits outside it. Left unfixed on Trey's ruling. Worth taking with whatever next touches that keyframe, alongside #433's note about tightening #117's guard.

436. [FIXED 2026-10-07] **A `new Set` DEDUPE IS A MEMBERSHIP COMPARISON WEARING DIFFERENT CLOTHES, AND THE RATCHET COULD NOT SEE IT.** `Array.from(new Set([...(op.team || []), personId]))` in the pending tray. A `Set` dedupes by `===`, so a member stored as the NUMBER 7 and added as the STRING "7" **both survive** and the same human ends up on the op twice. This is the person-id-type-drift family exactly — the silent kind, like `.includes(pid)` in #340 and #345 — and `scripts/_membership-lint.mjs` had been green over it since May, because the pattern it knew required `.includes(` sitting directly on `team`. The replacement is `withPerson(team, pid)` in `overlapRules.js`, which compares through `String`, returns the ORIGINAL array when nothing is added (so a drop on somebody already on the crew is not a change and does not register as a reassignment), and refuses a null/undefined person rather than storing one that `onTeam` could never match and nothing could ever remove. **EXTENDING THE RATCHET IS WHAT FOUND THE SECOND SITE, AND IT IS THE WORSE ONE.** `case "assign_person_to_job"` in the AI action handler carried the same raw `new Set`, and the line below it — `case "remove_person_from_job"` — carried the MIRROR, `team.filter(id => id !== input.person_id)`, which fails in the opposite direction: against a drifted type it removes NOBODY and reports success, so asking the assistant to take someone off a job leaves them on it. `input.person_id` arrives from the MODEL, which makes this the one place in the file where a person id has no provenance at all and the least reason to assume its type. Both now go through `withPerson`/`withoutPerson`. **THE `.map(String)` GUARD IS ALIVE FOR THIS PATTERN, WHICH IS WORTH RECORDING BECAUSE THE LINT'S OWN HEADER SAYS IT WAS REMOVED AS DEAD.** It was dead for `.includes`, where a `.map(String)` in between already prevented a match. For the `Set` form the normaliser sits on the ARRAY LITERAL, after the spreads and outside anything a regex can see — `[...new Set([...(panel.team || []), ...ops.flatMap(o => o.team || [])].map(String))]` in the job-detail roll-up is SAFE and was the first thing the extension flagged. A false positive on live code on the first file it ran against is how a guard stops being believed (LESSONS #4), so the array's extent is found by MATCHING BRACKETS rather than guessed at. The comma is the whole test: `new Set([...(op.team || [])])` with nothing added is a plain dedupe of one array, not a membership comparison, and is left alone. Baseline stays at 9 for TRAQS.jsx — the extension added two findings and both were fixed in the same pass rather than re-baselined. STILL OPEN, measured: **4 raw `team.filter(... !== ...)` removals remain** in TRAQS.jsx, which the ratchet does NOT catch; the removal side needs its own pattern, and catching it properly needs the member expression analysed rather than matched, which is the same parser the lint's header already records as the known gap. Covered by `scripts/pending-drop-test.mjs` sections 1, 1b and 2.

437. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT RULED] **PLACEMENT IMPLIES "Pending" IN EXACTLY ONE PLACE, AND "Pending" IS NOT A GUARANTEED STATUS.** `handlePendingItemDrop` promotes `"Not Started"` to `"Pending"` when a card is dropped. Nothing else in the product does: not `placeTaskAt`, not a drag-move, not the scheduler. MEASURED on Matrix: only **3 of 230** nodes are "Pending" while **181 of 226 DATED nodes are still "Not Started"**, so "scheduled therefore pending" is not how this org actually works and the tray is the outlier rather than the rule. TWO WAYS TO SETTLE IT, and they are opposite: either placement means Pending EVERYWHERE and the other three paths are missing it, or it means nothing anywhere and the tray should stop. Not ruled here, deliberately — #414 was a consolidation and removing the transition would have been a product change folded into one, which is the thing that pass existed to avoid. THE SECOND HALF IS A LATENT DEFECT REGARDLESS OF THE RULING: `statusOpts` is **user-editable** (`orgSettings.statusOpts` -> `STATUSES`), so `"Pending"` is not guaranteed to exist, and the old code wrote the literal unconditionally — an org that removed it would get a status its own dropdown cannot display and the user cannot select away from. #414 guards the write on `STATUSES.includes("Pending")` as a stopgap; the ruling still owes a proper answer, and if the answer is "placement means Pending" then the other three paths need it too and the guard needs a defined fallback rather than silently skipping. **RULED AND FIXED 2026-10-07: DROP THE PROMOTION.** Placing work on the schedule is not a status change — a drop says WHEN the work happens and WHO does it, and says nothing about whether it has begun, which is how every other placement path already behaved. THE EVIDENCE WAS GATHERED BEFORE THE RULING AND NONE OF IT SUPPORTED THE TRAY (`scripts/measure-pending-status.mjs`). Of the three nodes on Matrix carrying "Pending", **two are PANELS, which the old tray could never have promoted** — its panel branch wrote only `start`, `end` and `team`, never a status — and the third carries **53.03 logged hours while still saying Pending**, which is a stale hand-set label rather than a placement artefact. So at most one of the three could have come from the mechanism being removed, and the status the tray wrote is effectively invisible on the board. WHAT READS IT, checked repo-wide including the native clients, because "nothing reads it" is the kind of claim that needs a search rather than an impression: the health dot (`getHealth`), the panel roll-up (`getOpDisplayStatus`), a progress-percent cell, a status chart over a hardcoded five, the AI action schema, and **a "Pending" StatCard on the Team screen of BOTH iOS and Android**. None of them BREAKS — every one handles "Not Started" — so the ruling is about what they display, not about whether they work. The sharpest reader is logged separately as #440 and was the reason this was worth asking about rather than assuming. THE PASSTHROUGH WENT WITH IT, not just the call: `m.to.status` in `applyDragMove`, the `fromStatus`/`toStatus` pair in `moveLogEntry`, and the conditional `status` on `commitDates`' `from` all existed for exactly one caller, and that caller is the one being changed. A parameter no caller can set is #419's stranded control wearing a different hat, and a moveLog column nothing can write is a column of nulls. `scripts/pending-drop-test.mjs` section 5 is REWRITTEN AS A NEW RULE rather than the old one with its answer flipped (R3): it used to hold "the promotion is preserved because removing it is a product decision"; it now holds "moving work is not a status change, in any caller", and asserts that even a plan explicitly carrying a status cannot move one — so the removal is a rule in the code rather than an omission at the call site. 84 assertions, 11 mutants caught and 0 survived.

**A CORRECTION TO #414, RECORDED BECAUSE THE PROCESS FAILED EVEN THOUGH THE OUTCOME DID NOT.** #414's entry and commit both say the tray's behaviour was preserved exactly. For OPS it was. **For PANELS it was not**: the old code promoted only inside `if (item.kind === "op")`, and the consolidated version computed `promoted` from `node.status` with no kind check, so panel cards gained a promotion they had never had. That is a product change folded into a pass whose stated purpose was to avoid folding product changes into consolidations — the precise failure the entry claims to have guarded against. It costs nothing ONLY because the promotion was ruled away one turn later, which is luck rather than process: had the ruling gone the other way, a silent behaviour change would have shipped under a commit message asserting there wasn't one. The lesson is that "behaviour preserved exactly" is a claim needing an assertion per branch, not per function — the old code had two branches and the new code has one, and nothing in the suite noticed the arity change.

438. [FIXED 2026-10-07] **THE MOST EXPANSIVE GESTURE THE DEPARTMENT PICKER OFFERS PRODUCED ITS MOST RESTRICTIVE RESULT.** `normalizeDepartments(value, allKnown)` folds a set covering every known department down to `[]`, on the reasoning that "all of them" and "anyone" are the same statement. **That is true at the top and false everywhere below it**, because `[]` does not mean anyone — it means SAY NOTHING, and `unitDepartments` then asks the parent. So on an op under a panel that states "Wire": ticking every one of the org's 11 departments wrote `[]`, which resolved straight back to **Wire**. Someone opening a task up as wide as the control allows got it narrowed to one department instead, silently, with the picker then drawing it as unconstrained (#426). REACHABLE TODAY, not theoretical: Matrix has 11 roles configured and a panel stating "Admin" with three ops under it. FIXED ON THE DISTINCTION rather than by deleting the collapse, which is correct and wanted wherever nothing is above to fall through to: `departmentsAfterToggle` withholds `allRoles` from `normalizeDepartments` when `unitDepartments(null, panel, job)` is non-empty, so the collapse is offered only where `[]` would genuinely mean anyone. **The condition is derived from the tree, not passed as a flag** — a flag is something a future caller sets wrongly, and this one would be set wrongly by exactly the caller that most needs it right. The JOB counts as a constrained parent as well as the panel, which is its own mutant because reading only one level is the obvious half-fix. SECOND DEFECT FIXED IN THE SAME HELPER: the toggle started from the node's OWN set while the picker (post-#426) shows the EFFECTIVE one, so the first click on an inheriting op would have dropped the inherited department rather than adding to it — the control would have edited something other than what it displayed. Covered by `scripts/dept-inherit-test.mjs` sections 1 and 2; the "ticking everything under a constrained operation" case is the red proof.

439. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT BUILT — THE CONTROL NOW SAYS SO] **"ANYONE" IS UNREPRESENTABLE ON A NODE WHOSE PARENT STATES A DEPARTMENT.** `[]` is the only empty encoding and it already means "follow the parent", so there is no way to say "this task specifically may be done by anyone" underneath an operation that names a department. SAME SHAPE AS #428 AND #430 — one encoding carrying two facts — but worse in a way worth stating: there, both facts were expressible and the bug was that they shared a slot; here **one of the two facts cannot be written down at all**. Fixing it needs a third state in the data (`requiredDepartments: null` meaning inherit, distinct from `[]` meaning anyone) and that touches iOS, which reads `extras.text("requiredDepartment")` as a string at all three levels — the same dual-write constraint `withDepartmentDualWrite` already exists to satisfy. NOT BUILT, deliberately: it is a schema change and #426 was a presentation fix. **BUT THE CONTROL MUST NOT IMPLY THE OPTION EXISTS**, because the gesture a user reaches for first — untick the last box — appears to do nothing: the set empties, the parent's value comes straight back, and the picker redraws exactly as it was. So the dropdown states the limitation in place rather than leaving it to be discovered: *"Untick everything and this follows the operation (Admin). It can't be opened to anyone while the operation names one."* Shown only when there IS something above to inherit, so a top-level node — where `[]` does mean anyone and the sentence would be a lie — never shows it. The wording names the parent's ACTUAL departments rather than just its level, because "follows the operation" without saying what the operation requires answers half the question. When the schema change happens this line is what has to be removed, and it is deliberately specific enough that leaving it behind would be obviously wrong.

440. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT FIXED — DELIBERATELY] **THE HEALTH DOT REPORTS A DATA-ENTRY STATE, AND MOST OF THE BOARD IS ONE DAY FROM RED.** `getHealth` opens with a short-circuit: `if (t.status === "Not Started" && !started) return TD > t.start ? "critical" : "ontime";` — so a unit whose stored label still says "Not Started" and which has no logged hours goes **critical the day after its start date**, with no gradient and no regard for how much of its span remains. MEASURED on Matrix: of 182 dated schedulable units, **142 are already past their start with nothing logged** — 78% of the dated board sitting on, or one day from, a red dot. A five-day op placed today is green on day one and red on day two with four days left to run. THE INDICATOR IS NOT MEASURING WHAT IT CLAIMS TO. Red means "behind schedule" in its own tooltip, but what actually fires it is the absence of two data-entry events: somebody changing a dropdown, and somebody clocking in. The surrounding comment already records this hazard being hit once — *"a panel with 34.87h against it, 26% done and comfortably on schedule, read `critical` purely because the label still said Not Started"* — and `pctDoneOverride` was added to rescue exactly that case. It rescues the panel that HAS logged hours. It does nothing for the 142 that have none, which is the larger population by far. **A HEALTH INDICATOR THAT MOST OF THE BOARD TRIPS IS ONE PEOPLE LEARN TO IGNORE**, and an ignored indicator is worse than an absent one because it still occupies the space where a working one would go. NOT FIXED ON PURPOSE — Trey's call, logged to be visible rather than acted on, and any fix is a judgement about what "behind" should mean rather than a defect repair. THE TWO PATHS DIVERGE AS WELL, which matters to anyone who does take it on: `healthOf` (the Jobs list) supplies a real `pctDoneOverride`, so the status-keyed guesses never apply and a "Pending" unit with nothing logged is judged against `pctDone = 0`; `HealthIcon` calls `getHealth(t)` with no override, so the same unit is judged against `0.15`. The same op can be amber on one screen and green on another, and the direction is not even consistent — for a five-day span, "Pending" reads BEHIND on day one where "Not Started" reads ON TIME, then they converge to critical together on day two. That divergence, not the 0.15 constant, is what made #437's question hard to answer by reading. **CORRECTED 2026-10-07 BY THE MEASUREMENT (R2). THE ENTRY ABOVE BLAMES THE WRONG THING AND I WROTE IT WITHOUT CHECKING.** It says the indicator "is not measuring what it claims" and that red fires on "the absence of two data-entry events", and it frames the 142 as *one day from* red. Measured (`scripts/measure-health-dot.mjs`): **133 of 182 dated units are genuinely PAST THEIR END DATE and unfinished, with a median of 130 days and 75 of them more than 90 days past.** They are not one day from red, they are months beyond it, and **every red under today's rule is also red under a pure-calendar rule** — so the dot is not lying about any individual unit. The short-circuit I attacked fires on `TD > start`, but on this board almost everything that trips it has also passed its END, which is a fact about the calendar and not about a dropdown. **CHANGING THE RULE MOVES NOTHING.** Modelled over the live board: today 133 red; drop the short-circuit, 133; "late not behind" with red purely on the end date, 133; add a last-working-day amber, 133. Four different rules, one number — because the reds are real. THE REAL FINDING IS A LEVEL UP, AND IT IS NOT ABOUT THRESHOLDS: **110 of the 133 red units sit on a job in a terminal status** — 54 on `Finished`, 20 on `finished`, 17 on `Shipped/ invoiced`, plus Crated, Boxed up and the FAT states. The job shipped; its operations were never individually closed; and `getHealth` has no rollup, so it judges each op against a schedule that stopped being relevant the day the job left the building. Only **23** of the 133 are on a job that is still open. ASK THE PARENT AND THE NUMBER COLLAPSES: red goes 133 → **42** counting only Finished/Shipped as terminal, or → **23** counting the packaging and FAT states too, and the on-time KPI goes 27% → 74% or 85%. That is the whole difference between an indicator most of the board trips and one that points at twenty-three things. SEPARATELY, AND WORTH ITS OWN LOOK: the org's status list contains **`Finished` AND `finished` as two distinct entries** (17 jobs and 11 jobs), plus `Boxed up ` with a trailing space — so any rule keyed on a status string has to normalise, and anything that does not will silently treat a quarter of the closed jobs as open. The two-path divergence stands as described but is SMALL: 3 of 182 units disagree today, always `healthOf` saying ontime while `HealthIcon` says critical. **FIXED 2026-10-07, E-STRICT. AND THE CORRECTION ABOVE IS THE MORE USEFUL HALF OF THIS ENTRY.** The framing I filed — *"the dot reports a data-entry state rather than a work state"* — was wrong, and it survived a turn because Trey repeated it back to me, so neither of us was checking it. WHAT DISPROVED IT: modelling four rules over the live board. Today's rule, dropping the "Not Started" short-circuit, red purely on the end date, and red-plus-a-last-working-day-amber **all produce exactly 133 reds**. If the thresholds were the fault, moving them would move the number. The dot was never lying about a single unit; 133 of 182 are genuinely past their end date, median 130 days. THE FIX IS A ROLLUP, NOT A THRESHOLD: `healthState(unit, { today, jobStatus, pctDone })` in `src/health.js` asks whether the PARENT JOB is closed before it asks anything about the calendar, because 110 of the 133 sat on a job that had shipped. Red drops **133 → 42** and the on-time KPI goes **27% → 74%**, entirely by being more correct. E-STRICT RULED, and the narrowness is deliberate: `MTX FAT`, `C FAT`, `Crated`, `Boxed up` and `Ready for Packaging` probably mean the shop's work is done, and *probably* is how an indicator starts lying in the other direction. The broad set would take red to 23; it is available on evidence rather than ahead of it, and the suite ASSERTS each of those five is NOT closed, so widening is a deliberate edit against a failing test rather than a drift. THE `notyet` STATE IS NOT COSMETIC: 12 units had not reached their start date and were being counted as ON TIME in both the dashboard tile and the per-person employee figure — a KPI flattering itself, and on the employee page it is a number shown to an individual against their own name. `isJudged` excludes them from the measurement entirely rather than scoring them, because "has not begun" is not a verdict. `HealthIcon` renders nothing for it: a grey dot would read as a fourth verdict instead of the absence of one. THE TWO PATHS NOW COLLAPSE BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by agreement. `HealthIcon` took a node and called `getHealth` from module scope, where real progress is unreachable, so it fell through to the status-keyed guesses (0.5/0.15/0.25) the component path never reaches — the Jobs list and the dashboard showing different colours for the same operation. It takes `health` as a prop now; 12 call sites pass `healthOf(t)`; there is one computation and nothing left to agree. The old `getHealth` body is DELETED rather than parked beside the new one, since a second implementation nothing calls is #419's stranded control. `scripts/health-dot-test.mjs`, 67 assertions, 23 mutants caught and 0 survived.

441. [FIXED 2026-10-07] **A THEME TOGGLE IN A TAB YOU LEFT OPEN REVERTED YOUR COLUMN LAYOUT.** `user-settings.js` did `writeJson(key, stampObject(settings, existing))` — the body BECAME the blob. No merge, no ETag, no conditional put. The client sent every key on every write (its own comment said so: *"REPLACES THE BLOB WHOLESALE, so every preference has to be in here every time"*), and fetched the blob exactly ONCE per session: it is not in `sync.js`, not on Ably, not in IndexedDB. Those three facts compose into a data-loss bug nobody had named. **THE STALE WINDOW IS THE LIFETIME OF THE OLDER TAB, NOT THE 900ms DEBOUNCE.** Tabs A and B both load at 09:00; at 15:00 you widen a column in A, which writes all eight keys from A's state; at 16:00 you switch theme in B, which writes all eight keys from B's **09:00** state, and the column width is silently back to where it started. Nothing errors, nothing logs, and **the write that destroyed the edit was about a completely unrelated preference**. THE DIRECTION OF HARM IS THE WORST ONE AVAILABLE: the cheap, frequent writes (a theme, a toggle) destroy the expensive, rare state (a column layout is minutes of dragging and renaming). FIXED AS #339's SHAPE — the client sends only the keys that changed, the server merges them over what is stored — and **it is genuinely easier here than it was there**, which is worth stating because the two look identical from a distance. #339 needed an explicit `{ upsert, delete }` envelope because `reconcileDeletions` inferred deletion from ABSENCE, so a partial body read as a mass delete. **A preference is only ever SET, never removed**, so absence can simply mean "not mentioned" and a plain merge is sufficient: no envelope, no version flag, no delete list. NOT 409-ON-STALE, ruled explicitly: a conditional write is the stricter answer but it puts a conflict dialog in front of somebody who toggled a theme, which is worse than the bug it prevents. THE GUARD MOVED FROM SHAPE TO INTENT, exactly as the tasks guard did: "is the body empty" was a question about the REQUEST, and under a patch an empty body is a legitimate no-op, so it now asks whether the ACCOUNT ends up with nothing. It is **structurally unreachable** — a merge can only add keys, so the result is always a superset of what was stored — and it is kept rather than deleted because it is what stands between this endpoint and the 2026-06-03 shape if the semantics ever change back. `wouldEmptyPrefs` ignores `lastModifiedAt` on both sides, or a blob holding only a stamp would count as populated and the account's first real write would be refused; no fixture carried a stamp until a mutant walked straight through that branch. ONE CONSEQUENCE OF MERGING, FLAGGED RATHER THAN DISCOVERED LATER: a key no client sends any more is now KEPT instead of collected. The legacy per-user `statusOpts`/`priOpts` will linger in the blob indefinitely where the wholesale write used to delete them on the next preference change. Harmless — the fold that reads them returns null once every name is in the org list, and runs once per machine behind a localStorage flag — but two comments and an assertion in `org-options-test` said they were on a clock, and all three were corrected in place rather than left describing the old write (R3: the capture is still required, for a different reason — the bundle does not carry them, so without it they would never reach the org's list at all). `scripts/prefs-merge-test.mjs`, 125 assertions, 23 mutants caught and 0 survived; the red proof is the 09:00/15:00/16:00 sequence itself, played through both halves. A brittle assertion in `col-visibility-test` pinned the save effect's dep array as a LITERAL and went red the moment seven keys joined it — a true assertion failing for a reason it was not about, which is the fixed-width-slice problem in another costume; it asserts MEMBERSHIP now.

442. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, ANSWERED FROM SOURCE 2026-10-07 — reachable; urgency parked for an Android session] **THE TWO NATIVE CLIENTS DISAGREE ABOUT THE TYPE OF A PERSON ID, AND BOTH POST THE WHOLE ROSTER.** iOS declares `var id: String` (`Models.swift:880`); **Android declares `val id: Int = 0`** (`Models.kt:222`). Both have a full-roster POST — iOS `savePeople` (`APIService.swift:209`), Android `@POST("people")` wired through `AppState.kt:347`. iOS normalising numbers to strings is what caused #384: two records stored with numeric ids came back as `"99"` and `"100"`, missed the server's merge map, and lost their PINs, push tokens, active clock and PTO in one write. **Android would push them the other way.** #384's fix makes the server symmetric — it compares `String(p.id)` on both sides, so neither direction can miss any more — so this entry is about the CLIENTS still disagreeing, not about the server still being exposed. WHY IT STILL MATTERS WITH THE SERVER FIXED: the id that gets STORED is whatever the last writer sent, so the roster's id types will keep flipping between string and number depending on which client saved last. Everything downstream that compares a person id raw — the nine sites the new ratchet baselines, plus any native code doing the same — sees a type that changes under it for reasons unrelated to anything the user did. **ANSWERED 2026-10-07 BY READING THE KOTLIN, AND IT IS THE BAD ANSWER.** It does not throw. `ApiService.kt:20` registers a `SafeIntDeserializer` for both `Int` and boxed `Integer`: `try { json.asInt } catch (_: Exception) { 0 }`. A `t…` token id therefore does not fail the decode — **it silently becomes `0`**. On Matrix that is **16 of 18 people sharing `id = 0`**, with only Treysen (`"99"` -> 99) and Max (`"100"` -> 100) decoding to anything real. The deserializer exists so one bad field cannot drop a whole response, and it does that; what it also does is turn every unparseable id into the same value. REACHABLE, AND THE WORST PATH IS THE SIMPLEST ONE. `AppState.kt:345` `updatePeople` POSTs the whole roster, and `TeamScreen.kt` calls it twice — an edit at `:357` and a **DELETE at `:630`**: `updatePeople(people.filter { it.id != person.id })`. With sixteen people sharing id 0, **deleting any one of them filters out all sixteen**, and the POST arrives missing them; `reconcileDeletions` tombstones every one. Sixteen people removed by one tap, for a caller holding `manageTeam`. The edit path has the same shape: sixteen records arrive as id 0, the merge maps them onto one key, and the fifteen stored records they no longer match are absent from the incoming set and tombstoned too. **THE MITIGATION IS NOT IN THE CODE, IT IS IN THE CALENDAR** — the Android app is still in development and is not in anyone's hands, so none of this has run against production. That is the only thing standing between it and a roster wipe, and it is not a guard. WHAT A BUILD AND AN EMULATOR WOULD ADD, parked for the Android session: whether the Team screen renders usably with sixteen identical ids (a list that looks obviously broken is one nobody reaches the delete button on), and whether any build has been installed beyond the dev machine. Both change the URGENCY; neither changes the fix. **BUILT 2026-10-07 — UNCOMPILED, parked for an Android build, and it ships on its own.** `Person.id` is a `String`, which is what the id format actually is: `uid()` tokens like `t0gnvtljt`, with two legacy numeric accounts. The change is wider than one field because **person ids are not only in `Person`**: `team` on Op, Panel and Job was `List<Int>` and collapsed every membership to zero by the same route; so did `participantIds` (message threads), `memberIds` (groups), `jobTeamIds`/`newTeamIds` (the assign payload), `currentPersonId`, and nine `personId: Int` parameters across the API and AppState surfaces. `workDays`, `payDates` and the theme id stay `Int` — they are numbers, not ids. **TWO REAL BUGS FELL OUT OF THE SWEEP RATHER THAN THE ORIGINAL REPORT.** `MessagesScreen` parsed a DM thread key with `split("_").mapNotNull { it.toIntOrNull() }`, so every token id was DROPPED and a direct message with anyone but the two numeric accounts resolved to no person and fell through to the literal "Direct Message". It would also have stopped compiling the moment `id` became a String, which is how it surfaced. And `currentPersonId: Int?` meant the signed-in user matched nobody. Neither was in this entry as filed. **THE DELETE NEEDED NO CODE CHANGE AT ALL**, which is worth stating because it was the headline symptom: `filter { it.id != person.id }` is correct Kotlin and always was — it removed sixteen people only because sixteen ids were the same number. Give it real ids and it removes one. **WHAT THE BUILD CONFIRMS vs WHAT THE SOURCE PROVES:** the source proves no person id is typed `Int` anywhere any more, and `scripts/native-id-status-test.mjs` asserts that in `npm run build`. The build confirms it COMPILES and that no call site passes the wrong type; only an emulator answers whether the Team screen was ever usable enough for anyone to reach the delete. **A JS suite cannot compile Kotlin or run the app, and this entry claims neither.** THE FIX, when someone can test it, is to make `Person.id` a String on Android to match iOS and the server's own storage; `Int` cannot represent the id format this product actually uses, so it is wrong independently of the drift.

443. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT A DEFECT IN ANY CODE — a data state nobody is watching] **THE SET OF PEOPLE WHO MAY CLOCK IN IS NOT THE SET WHO CAN.** Measured on Matrix: 18 live people, 6 carrying a PIN, 6 with `canClockInOut: true` — and **they are not the same six**. `App Review` has `canClockInOut: true` and **no PIN**, so the permission says yes and the kiosk cannot authenticate them. `Andrew` has a PIN and `canClockInOut: false`, so the credential exists and the gate refuses it. Neither is a bug in any code path: both states are reachable by an admin setting one field and not the other, and nothing in the product relates them. THAT IS THE POINT — **the two facts are edited in different places and no screen shows them together**, so the mismatch is invisible until somebody stands at the kiosk at the start of a shift and cannot start it. This is the same shape as #384's consequence without #384's cause: a clock-in that fails for a reason the person cannot see and cannot fix themselves. WORTH A CHECK RATHER THAN A RULE, because the right answer is not obvious: a PIN with no permission is harmless and may be deliberate (someone who clocked in last month and had the permission withdrawn), while a permission with no PIN is a person who will be stopped. Candidates, not proposed: surface `hasPin` beside the `canClockInOut` toggle in the Employees editor — the server already computes and returns that flag — or a one-line warning on the Time Clock settings page listing anyone who may clock in and cannot. Found while measuring #384; it is not that defect and does not share its mechanism.

444. [FIXED 2026-10-07, one deliberate exception] **THE ASYMMETRY THAT BROKE `people.js`, EVERYWHERE ELSE IT STILL LIVED.** #384's ratchet found nine more sites of the shape. They were not one group, and sorting them was most of the work. **ASYMMETRIC — THREE, ALL IN `dragMove.js`, AND IDENTICAL TO THE DEFECT:** `shares` (`:157`), `moverIds` (`:161`) and `byId` (`:263`) were each keyed on a RAW mover id and probed through `sid()` — `byId.get(sid(node.id))`, `shares.has(sid(u.id))`, and `excludeIds.has(id)` in `overlapRules.js:146` where `id` is `String(o.unit.id)`. Safe only because every caller happens to build movers with `id: String(op.id)`, **which is exactly the condition that stopped holding in `people.js`**. `moverIds` is a `Set`, so the ratchet never saw it at all — found by reading the file the ratchet pointed at, not by the ratchet. THE RED PROOF IS BEHAVIOURAL, not a source assertion: a mover built with a numeric id did not move its node, logged nothing, and the refusal path treated it as an obstacle to itself — all silent. **CROSS-BOUNDARY — TWO, both symmetric in form and one normalisation from not being:** `pinById` in the Settings PIN reveal keys from the SERVER ROSTER and probes from the SETTINGS DRAFT, and those are PERSON ids, the only ones measured actually drifting; `scheduledMap` in the replan keys from `newSubs` and probes from `p.subs`, while the very next line already does `String(fresh.id)` for a Set — the file disagreed with itself inside four lines. **LOCAL — THREE**, built and consumed in one function from one array: `jobDetail.js`'s `idMap` and TRAQS.jsx's `jobMap`. Fixed anyway, because the cost is nothing and "it cannot drift from here" is an argument that has now failed twice. `duplicateJob` got a behavioural test while it was open: a job with numeric ids and a numeric dep now duplicates with the dep remapped onto its new id rather than left dangling. **ONE IS LEFT, AND IT IS AN EXCEPTION RATHER THAN AN OVERSIGHT.** `getDepGroup`'s `adj` (`TRAQS.jsx:9893`) feeds a Set that is RETURNED and then probed raw by three call sites — `depGroupIds.has(s.id)`, `.has(op.id)` and a `forEach`. Stringifying the map without changing what the Set carries is correct but subtle, and `getDepGroup` sits inside the component where no suite can drive it to prove the rewrite behaved. Trey's ruling, and the right one: **a known exception the ratchet carries beats one quietly fixed into something else.** If it is ever taken on, the thing to preserve is that `group` keeps the ORIGINAL id values. Baseline drops **9 → 1** and names it. MEASURED, and it is the same "latent" status every entry in this family has: all 1,460 live node ids and all 6 dep ids on Matrix are strings, so none of these is firing today. Neither was `people.js`, until a client normalised two numbers and it lost two PINs, two push tokens, an active clock and an approved PTO entry in one write. `scripts/id-key-symmetry-test.mjs`, 26 assertions, 15 mutants caught and 0 survived — including one that raises the baseline back to 9, because a ratchet whose number can be edited upward to hide a regression is not a ratchet.

445. [PARTLY FIXED 2026-10-07 — the rule that needed it; the data migration is still owed] **`Finished` AND `finished` ARE TWO DIFFERENT STATUSES, AND 81 COMPARISONS CANNOT SEE THE SECOND ONE.** `statusOpts` is a user-editable list, and 11 of Matrix's 28 finished jobs carry the lowercase spelling — **39% of the finished work**. **CORRECTED 2026-10-07 WHEN THE CURE WAS SCOPED (R2): THE DUPLICATE IS IN THE DATA, NOT IN THE LIST.** This entry said the org "holds both spellings as separate entries". It does not — `statusOpts` has 17 entries and **no collisions at all**; only `Finished` is in it. So the 11 jobs carry a status the dropdown cannot display and the user cannot select away from, which is a worse state than a duplicated option and a different fix. The same scoping found a second orphan the entry missed: **2 jobs are `Procurement` while the list spells it `Procurment`** — also unselectable, also invisible to every comparison. THE DAMAGE IS CONFINED TO JOBS: all 118 panels and all 112 ops match the list exactly, so the merge is **13 job records and one list entry**, not 28 and not the whole tree. Every `status === "Finished"` in the product is blind to them: they do not fade on the gantt (`barPaint.js`), are not skipped by the schedulers (`statsMath.js`), are not excluded from the overlap rules (`overlapRules.js`), are not treated as closed by the permission check (`taskActions.js`) or by the timeclock, and do not count as done in any KPI. Nothing errors, nothing logs, and the jobs look open on every screen that matters. MEASURED: **81 raw comparisons across 7 files** — `TRAQS.jsx` 69, `timeclock.js` 3, `barPaint.js` 2, `dragMove.js` 2, `statsMath.js` 2, `taskActions.js` 2, `overlapRules.js` 1. WHAT WAS BUILT: `src/statusText.js` with `normalizeStatus` (trim, collapse inner whitespace, lowercase), `sameStatus`, and `isClosedStatus`, used by the health rule that needed it (#440), plus a `statusLiteralViolations` ratchet baselined at those 81 so the eighty-second fails the build. The two new modules are held at ZERO directly rather than by a baseline that could absorb a regression. The ratchet is deliberately scoped to the editable vocabulary — `req.status === "approved"` and finish-request statuses are server-written lowercase enums no user can retype, and flagging them would make the guard unusable (LESSONS #4). **WHAT IS STILL OWED, AND IT IS THE REAL CURE: THE DATA, NOT 81 EDITS.** Two changes, neither done here: (1) the status editor should refuse to create an entry that normalises to one already in the list, so the duplicate cannot be made again; (2) a one-time normalising pass over stored statuses and over `statusOpts` itself, merging `finished` into `Finished` and trimming `Boxed up `. That is a write against live production data and belongs to its own ruling with its own backup — the same bar the 2026-06-03 incident set. Until it happens, 81 comparisons remain wrong on 11 jobs, and the ratchet's purpose is to stop that number growing rather than to pretend it is small.

446. [SCOPED 2026-10-07, NOT BUILT — needs a ruling and a backup] **THE STATUS DATA CURE.** Three parts, in order, none of them done. **(1) THE EDITOR REFUSES A COLLIDING ENTRY.** `statusOpts` must not be able to hold two names that `normalizeStatus` reduces to one, nor one with leading/trailing padding — the add/rename path trims and rejects a duplicate rather than creating the state the merge then has to clean up. Cheap, no data touched, and it is what stops this recurring. **(2) THE ONE-TIME PASS**, and it is far smaller than the entry it comes from claimed: **13 job records** — 11 `finished` → `Finished`, 2 `Procurement` LEFT AS THEY ARE and **the LIST corrected to match them** — ruled 2026-10-07: merge toward the correct spelling. The org's entry `Procurment` is the typo and the two jobs happen to be right, so this is one list edit rather than two record edits, and it is the only direction that does not preserve a misspelling because it got there first — plus **one `statusOpts` entry**, `Boxed up ` → `Boxed up`. **All 118 panels and all 112 ops already match the list exactly**, so the tree below job level is untouched. NOTE THE COUPLING: trimming `Boxed up ` in the LIST without trimming the one stored job that matches it exactly would turn a matching status into an orphan — the two edits are a single operation, not two. **(3) THE BAR, which is 2026-06-03's:** S3 versioning is on, so the precondition is a captured version id for `tasks.json` and `settings.json` before anything is written, a DRY RUN printed and shown first (every record, its old and new value), the write via the normal POST path so the empty-overwrite guard and timestamping apply, and a re-read afterwards proving the orphan count is zero. **WHAT ELSE STORES A STATUS STRING — surveyed, because a merge that fixes `tasks.json` and misses a second store just moves the problem.** Checked and CLEAN: `exportTemplates` (1 template, **0** nodes carry a status — `loadTemplate` writes `"Not Started"` explicitly); conditional-formatting `conditions` (**0** configured, and `evalCondition`'s `equals` already compares case-insensitively); the moveLog (carries no status at all since #437 removed the passthrough); `rule-events` (logs ids and fields, not statuses). Checked and NOT CLEAN, each needing its own decision: **THE AI SCHEMA** pins `enum: ["Not Started","Pending","In Progress","On Hold","Finished"]` — the five DEFAULTS — so the assistant cannot set 12 of this org's 17 statuses and does not know they exist; the enum should be built from `statusOpts` rather than hardcoded. **iOS IS THE SERIOUS ONE AND IT IS NOT A MERGE PROBLEM — see #447.**

447. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT FIXED — found while scoping #446] **iOS NARROWS EVERY JOB STATUS TO FIVE AND WRITES THE NARROWED VALUE BACK.** `Models.swift` declares `enum JobStatus: String { notStarted, pending, inProgress, onHold, finished }` — the five defaults — and decodes with `status = (try? c.decode(JobStatus.self, forKey: .status)) ?? .notStarted`. **Anything outside those five silently becomes `.notStarted`.** On Matrix, which has 17 statuses, that is `Shipped/ invoiced`, `MTX FAT`, `C FAT`, `Crated`, `Boxed up`, `Procurement`, `Shipped not invoiced` and the lowercase `finished` — **38 of 64 jobs, 59% of the board, all reading "Not Started" on the phone**. THE READ IS THE SMALL HALF. `encode` writes `try c.encode(status, forKey: .status)` from the enum, and `saveJobs` re-encodes the whole `[Job]` array and POSTs it (`AppState.swift:2178`, reached from `updateJob`). **So one job edit from iOS rewrites 38 jobs' statuses to "Not Started" on the server**, destroying the real value for every client. SAME FAMILY AS #384 AND #442: a native client's narrower type silently destroying server data on a round-trip — `JobStatus` is to `status` what Android's `Int` is to a person id. AND THE FILE NEXT DOOR ALREADY KNOWS: `JSONPassthrough.swift` exists precisely because "`saveJobs` re-encodes the whole `[Job]` array and POSTs it, and `tasks.js` replaces the object rather than merging field by field — so every key Swift did not model is DESTROYED the moment anyone edits a cell." That mechanism protects UNKNOWN keys. `status` is a KNOWN key narrowed by an enum, so it falls outside the protection by exactly the route the comment describes. THE FIX IS THE SAME SHAPE AS ANDROID'S: `status` should be a `String` on the Swift model — the vocabulary is org-editable and no fixed enum can represent it — with the enum kept only where a known value is genuinely needed and compared through a normalising helper. **ANSWERED 2026-10-07 BY READING THE SWIFT — IT IS LIVE, AND BOTH ANSWERS ARE WORSE THAN THE QUESTIONS.** (1) IS A STATUS EDIT REACHABLE? Yes, and it is not a picker behind a screen — it is a TAP. `TasksView.swift:2533` renders `StatusBadge(status: job.status)` with `.onTapGesture { if mayEdit { apply(next(after: job.status)) } }`, cycling `private static let cycle: [JobStatus] = [.pending, .inProgress, .finished]`, gated only on `can(.editJobs)`. One tap on a job row's status badge sets that job to one of THREE values, destroying whatever it really was. (2) DOES THE RE-ENCODE NEED A STATUS EDIT? No — **any iOS job write does it**, which makes the badge almost beside the point. `updateJob`/`updateJobs` set `jobs = newJobs` and call `scheduleSave()`, which after a 1s debounce runs `persistJobs()` → `api.saveJobs(jobs)` — **the entire array**. Four view call sites reach it (`TasksView`, `JobEditView`, `JobDetailView`, `AddJobSheet`), plus undo and redo. So adding a job, editing its details, assigning someone, or pressing undo all re-encode and POST every job, and **38 of 64 flatten to "Not Started" on the way out**. The status screen is irrelevant: the destruction rides on the save, not on the edit. PANELS AND OPS DECLARE THE SAME ENUM (`Models.swift:264`, `:426`) and are at risk by the same route, but are SAFE TODAY BY COINCIDENCE: every panel and op status in use at Matrix — Finished, In Progress, Pending, Not Started — happens to fall inside iOS's five. Only jobs have drifted outside it. THE FIX, SCOPED: `status` becomes a plain `String` on Job/Panel/Op, decoded and re-encoded verbatim, which is the passthrough's behaviour applied to a key Swift happens to model. That alone stops the destruction. The enum is kept where a narrow value is genuinely wanted, derived as `JobStatus(rawValue: status)` and OPTIONAL — nil for the twelve it cannot name — because it IS load-bearing in four places: `StatusBadge` (label and colour), `AnalyticsView` (`JobStatus.allCases` drives a status breakdown chart, plus `== .finished` / `== .inProgress`), `ClientsView` (three `== .inProgress` filters), and the cycle control itself. **THE CYCLE CONTROL IS A PRODUCT DECISION AND IT STILL DESTROYS DATA AFTER THE TYPE CHANGE**: it can offer three of seventeen, so tapping it on a `Shipped/ invoiced` job overwrites a status the phone cannot express. Either it goes, or iOS has to learn the org's real list — and **iOS NEVER FETCHES `statusOpts`**: it sits in `OrgSettings`' `JSONExtras` passthrough, unmodelled, so the phone cannot currently know the other twelve exist. WHAT A MAC BUILD WOULD ADD, parked for when Trey is on one: confirmation that the Tasks list is reachable in the shipped build and that `can(.editJobs)` is true for a normal admin — that is CONFIRMATION of a control the source shows is wired, not discovery. Nothing in the scoping above waits on it. **BUILT 2026-10-07 — UNCOMPILED. THIS MUST NOT SHIP UNTIL IT HAS BEEN BUILT ON A MAC, and it goes out on its own rather than riding in a batch with web work.** THE FIX STORES THE RAW STRING AND DERIVES THE ENUM, which is deliberately NOT the literal shape discussed, and the deviation is the engineering judgement worth recording. Making `status` a `String` outright would have touched **~60 read sites across 20 files** — `$0.status == .finished` and friends — in a language this machine cannot compile, and handing over 60 unverifiable Swift edits is worse than the bug. The DESTRUCTION lives entirely in decode and encode, so: `statusRaw: String` becomes the stored property, decoded and encoded verbatim, and `status` becomes a COMPUTED property over it with a getter (`JobStatus(rawValue:) ?? .notStarted`) and a SETTER (`statusRaw = newValue.rawValue`). Every read site and every `p.status = .inProgress` write site keeps working untouched. **THE SETTER IS THE LOAD-BEARING HALF** — without it, assigning through the enum would leave `statusRaw` stale and the next encode would write the old value, which is the same destruction with the sign flipped. `statusKnown: JobStatus?` is added for code that must tell "iOS does not model this" from "it is Not Started", which the non-optional view cannot say. All three models — Op, Panel and Job — got it together: panels and ops were safe only because every status in use at Matrix happens to fall inside iOS's five, and **safe by coincidence is the condition that stopped holding in people.js**. THE DISPLAY HALF IS THEN SMALL RATHER THAN SIXTY: `StatusBadge` takes the raw string and prints it verbatim, with the COLOUR still derived from the enum and a neutral chip for anything unmodelled — a guessed colour on a status the phone cannot name would be a second lie on top of the one just fixed. Five call sites now pass `statusRaw`. **THE CYCLE CONTROL IS GONE, RULED** — and the LONG-PRESS PICKER with it, which is a decision beyond the literal ruling and is flagged here rather than buried: the ruling was "no status edit beats one that can only produce three answers", the picker offered `JobStatus.allCases` which is FIVE of seventeen, and the same sentence covers it. Both gestures are removed rather than widened, because widening needs iOS to know the org's list and it does not — `statusOpts` sits unmodelled in `OrgSettings`' passthrough and is never fetched. `JobStatusBadge` is kept as a display-only wrapper so the call sites do not move and the next person to want an editable badge finds the note instead of the loop. WHAT THE MAC BUILD CONFIRMS vs WHAT THE SOURCE ALREADY PROVES: the source proves the stored/encoded value is now the server's own string and that no decode narrows it — that is textual and `scripts/native-id-status-test.mjs` asserts it in `npm run build`. The build confirms it COMPILES and that no SwiftUI view broke on the `StatusBadge` signature change. **A JS suite cannot compile Swift, type-check it, or run the app, and this entry claims none of those.** **#446's merge must not run before this is decided** — normalising 13 records is pointless if a phone can flatten 38 the next time somebody taps save.

448. [LOGGED 2026-10-07, NOT FIXED — #447's shape from a different client] **THE AI CAN ONLY SET FIVE OF SEVENTEEN STATUSES, AND DOES NOT KNOW THE OTHER TWELVE EXIST.** `TRAQS.jsx:437` pins the tool schema to `status: { type: "string", enum: ["Not Started","Pending","In Progress","On Hold","Finished"] }` — the DEFAULTS, hardcoded, rather than the org's `statusOpts`. Matrix has seventeen.
