476. [MEASURED 2026-10-08; NOT BUILT, awaiting a ruling] **THE FINISH-REQUEST CLUSTER: THE DATA IS CLEAN, THE RULE IS DUPLICATED, AND THE SHARED HELPER IS THE LESS COMPLETE OF THE TWO WRITERS.**

**ONE. HOW MANY REPRESENTATIONS SURVIVE: effectively one, and this is a quiet answer stated plainly rather than treated as no result.** Root cause 2d's collapse HELD. Across 582 live nodes: **16** carry a `finishRequests[]`, **22** entries in total, **0 pending** (17 approved, 5 declined); **0** nodes carry `pendingFinish === true` (23 carry the key, every one false); **0** carry the deprecated singular `finishRequest` pointer; **0** carry `pendingSession`. `pendingFinishOf()` and the raw mirror **disagree on zero nodes** — the 10-out-of-11 disagreement rate in `finishRequests.js`'s own header is gone. Entry shape is complete: all **22/22** carry `id`, `at`, `by`, `byName`, `status`, `resolvedBy`, `resolvedByName` and `resolvedAt`, so **0 of 22 resolved entries fail to record who resolved them or when**. The three-representation problem is closed in the DATA.

**TWO. THE RULE EXISTS TWICE, AND NOTHING CHECKS THEY AGREE.** `src/finishRequests.js` `pendingFinishOf` is the web and server's; iOS has its own 210-line `Services/CompletionRequestRules.swift` with `status(target:requestId:)`. They answer deliberately different questions — the web asks "is any request open on this op", iOS asks "what is the status of THIS request id" — and iOS documents one intentional departure (an unfound target stays `nil`/unknown rather than falling back to "pending", because a wrong "pending" renders Approve/Deny buttons that do nothing). That is a defensible pair, not a bug, and it is recorded here because **no test exercises both against the same fixture**, so the next change to either is unguarded. **ONE THING THAT IS NOT A RISK, checked rather than assumed: iOS's `FinishRequestEntry` models a SUPERSET of what the web writes** — it adds `declineReason` — so #458's narrowing-client mechanism does not apply to this field. iOS also writes all three forms when raising (`addPendingRequest` sets the stamp, the mirror and the list row), so a current-build iOS request is complete on arrival.

**THREE. DOES AN iOS-RAISED, WEB-APPROVED REQUEST AGREE ABOUT WHAT HAPPENED? ON *WHETHER*, YES. ON *WHO*, IT DEPENDS WHICH WEB BUTTON WAS PRESSED — AND THE SHARED HELPER IS THE WRONG ONE.** There are two approval surfaces, already known to have diverged once on placement, which is why `finishedOpFields` was extracted. That extraction fixed placement and **explicitly left the finish-request bookkeeping unshared** — its own comment says the list is "the chat path's own bookkeeping and it merges them itself" — and that is exactly where they still disagree:

| | `adminApproveJobFinish` (chat bubble) | `approveFinish` (schedule / job details) |
|---|---|---|
| writer | its own `resolveReq`/`declineReq` | the SHARED `resolveRequest` |
| `resolvedBy`, `resolvedAt` | yes | yes |
| **`resolvedByName`** | **yes** | **NO** |
| **`declineReason`** | **yes** | **NO** |
| no list row exists | **UPSERTS one** | **`.map()` — closes it with NO record at all** |

The upsert is the sharper half. The chat path carries a comment explaining why it was changed: *"A request that never had a finishRequests[] row — an iOS request writes only pendingFinish — matched nothing here, so approving it recorded who approved it NOWHERE."* `resolveRequest` still does `(op?.finishRequests || []).map(...)`, so on exactly that record it produces an empty list, sets the mirror false, clears the pointer, and **leaves no evidence the approval ever happened**. The repair was applied to the bespoke path and never to the shared one. **LIVE IMPACT TODAY IS ZERO AND THE MEASUREMENT SAYS SO:** all 22 entries carry `resolvedByName`, which only the chat path writes, so **every resolution in Matrix's history came through the chat bubble and `approveFinish` has never resolved a request in production**. It is live, reachable code on two buttons (`:21767`, `:22402`), not a dead branch — latent, not realised. A corroborating detail: **no entry carries `declineReason`**, so all 5 declines were made through the chat path without a reason typed, which is consistent with the same conclusion.

**A UI WORKAROUND IS ALREADY COMPENSATING FOR IT.** The finish-request card at `:23374` says *"resolvedByName is written on every decision"* — which is false for `approveFinish` — and then falls back to scanning the list for any row that has one. The comment states the invariant the code immediately works around.

**PROPOSED, NOT BUILT.** Make `resolveRequest` the single writer and give it what the chat path has: take `byName` and an optional `reason`, UPSERT rather than map so a mirror-only request still records its approver, and have `adminApproveJobFinish` call it instead of its own `resolveReq`/`declineReq`. Then the `:23374` fallback can go, and the comment can state an invariant that is true. **THE ONE DECISION THAT IS NOT MINE:** the chat path's upsert invents an entry id and copies `by`/`byName` from the deprecated `finishRequest` stamp when no row exists. With 0 such records live and current iOS writing the row, that branch may be dead weight worth deleting rather than porting — but it is the only thing standing behind a legacy record nobody has touched since, and deleting it is a judgement about whether those records still exist anywhere outside Matrix. **WHAT THIS CLUSTER DOES NOT TOUCH:** #460's approval-card hours source and #463's unpinned fields are separate and both already resolved on their own terms — #463 by `TASK_CONFLICT_MODE=enforce` covering `status`, `pendingFinish` and the list, which is a FLAG rather than a line of code and reopens if anyone unsets it. `scripts/measure-finish-requests.mjs`. **BUILT 2026-10-08 — `resolveRequest` IS THE SINGLE WRITER.** It now takes `byName` and an optional `reason`, and UPSERTS rather than maps. `adminApproveJobFinish` and `declineJobFinish` lost their own `resolveReq`/`declineReq` and call it; the schedule surface's two call sites pass a name. All four call sites now pass `byName`, and the suite pins that **exactly**, per site — see the mutation note below for why a count was not enough.

**THE SHAPE THIS ENTRY EXISTS TO NAME: A PARTIAL EXTRACTION.** This is not two implementations that grew apart, which is the shape the rest of the campaign has been about (#384, #349, #458, the four theme ladders, the two `loadTemplate`s). It is **ONE EXTRACTION THAT DELIBERATELY TOOK HALF.** `finishedOpFields` was pulled out of these two approval surfaces to fix a real placement divergence, and its comment states the carve-out plainly: *"It deliberately does not touch finishRequest or finishRequests: those are the chat path's own bookkeeping and it merges them itself."* The extraction fixed what it took and **froze the boundary around what it left** — and the half left behind kept diverging for another two root causes. **THE DIAGNOSTIC THAT MAKES IT WORTH RECOGNISING: THE SHARED HELPER ENDED UP THE LESS COMPLETE OF THE TWO WRITERS.** Repairs land where the bug is reported, and the bug was always reported against the chat bubble, so the chat path accumulated the upsert, the name and the reason while `resolveRequest` — the thing named as the single representation — got none of them. A reader auditing this would see a shared helper and two callers and conclude the collapse was done. **So the smell is not "two implementations"; it is a shared helper that is thinner than the bespoke code still sitting beside it.** When an extraction documents what it is NOT taking, that sentence is a defect waiting to be written, and the next person should treat it as one.

**THE UPSERT IS PORTED, AND LOGGED AS SUSPECTED DEAD WITH THE EVIDENCE RATHER THAN THE GUESS.** It fires only when no pending row matched and `pendingFinishOf` is still true — a request whose only trace is the mirror, which an old iOS build produced by writing `pendingFinish` and no row. **IT CANNOT FIRE AT MATRIX TODAY:** 0 nodes carry `pendingFinish === true`, 0 carry the deprecated singular pointer, all 22 resolved entries have a row, and current iOS writes all three forms when it raises (`CompletionRequestRules.addPendingRequest`). It was kept anyway, and the reasoning is Trey's: the judgement is about the orgs that are **not** Matrix. This is a multi-tenant product with one customer, and a branch that costs nothing and stands behind a legacy record is cheaper than discovering it was needed. Whoever revisits this should delete it only after checking the same four numbers against every org, not just this one.

**THE `:23362` WORKAROUND IS GONE AND ITS COMMENT IS NOW TRUE.** It asserted *"resolvedByName is written on every decision"* and then, in the next line, scanned the item for the most recent row that happened to have one — the comment stated the invariant while the code beneath it worked around the invariant being false. The card reads the request's own row now. The lookup of `resolvedBy` in `people` stays, for rows written before any name was recorded.

**RED FIRST, 13 FAILING ASSERTIONS.** 71 assertions, 14 mutants, 14 caught, 0 survived. **TWO SURVIVED THE FIRST RUN AND BOTH WERE MY ASSERTIONS MEASURING THE POPULATION INSTEAD OF THE SITE:** "at least three call sites pass a name" survived a mutant that stripped the name from one of the four, because the other three still matched. Replaced with an exact count of four, a check that all four carry `byName`, and one pinned regex per surface. A threshold over a set of call sites is not an assertion about any of them.

**THE PARITY FIXTURE: WORTH BUILDING, NARROWLY, AND HALF OF IT NEEDS THE MAC.** The `schedule-parity` pattern is: the JS computes expected values from the real source into `fixtures/schedule-parity.json`, the JS suite fails if the JS drifts, and `ScheduleParityTests` fails on the same file if the Swift drifts — neither side can move alone. **IT DOES NOT FIT AS-IS, because that pattern compares PORTS — the same function on both sides — and these two are deliberately different functions:** `pendingFinishOf(op) -> Bool` asks "is any request open on this op"; `CompletionRequestRules.status(target:requestId:) -> String?` asks "what is the status of THIS request". **IT FITS AFTER ONE REFRAMING:** both sides can answer a request-level projection — *given this op and this request id, is it pending, approved, declined, or unknown* — which the web expresses as "the entry's status, else `pendingFinishOf` ⇒ pending, else null". Then the fixture's job is exactly what makes it worth doing: **the documented departure stops being prose and becomes a per-case field**, so iOS returning `nil` for an unfound target instead of "pending" is one row marked as intended rather than a paragraph nobody diffs. COST: ~15 cases covering the 11 historical combinations plus the mirror-only and unfound-target ones; ~100 lines of JS generator and checker modelled line-for-line on `schedule-parity-test.mjs`; ~70 lines of Swift decoding the same file. **I CAN BUILD THE FIXTURE, THE JS HALF AND THE SWIFT SOURCE; I CANNOT RUN THE SWIFT HALF FROM THIS MACHINE**, so it would ship with the same "correct and uncompiled" caveat as #447 and the Mac build would answer whether `ScheduleParityTests` passes. WHAT TIPS IT TOWARDS YES: the handshake this guards runs ACROSS the two platforms — raised on iOS, approved on the web — which is the one place a divergence is guaranteed to be user-visible, and `schedule-parity`'s own header already names a drifting second implementation as the shape behind most of this campaign's defects. WHAT ARGUES FOR KEEPING IT SMALL: the two rules AGREE on every live record today (0 disagreements across 582 nodes), so this buys protection against a future change rather than fixing a present fault.


477. [BUILT 2026-10-08, with the Swift half UNCOMPILED] **THE FINISH-REQUEST STATUS RULE IS NOW PINNED ACROSS BOTH PLATFORMS, AND THE FIXTURE FOUND A SECOND DEPARTURE THE PROSE HAD NEVER RECORDED.** `fixtures/finish-parity.json`, 18 cases, written by `scripts/finish-parity-test.mjs` from `src/finishRequests.js` and read by `FinishParityTests.swift`. **IT IS NOT THE `schedule-parity` PATTERN AS-IS, and the difference is the whole design.** That fixture compares PORTS — one function written twice — and fails if either side moves. These two are deliberately DIFFERENT functions: `requestStatusOf(target, requestId) -> String`, which never returns null because it always guesses, against `CompletionRequestRules.status(target:requestId:) -> String?`, which returns nil for "unknown". So this fixture does not assert agreement. **IT ASSERTS THAT THE DOCUMENTED DEPARTURE IS THE ONLY DIFFERENCE**, by carrying iOS's expected answer as a per-case field beside the web's, with `iosDiffers` and a `why` the generator REFUSES to let you omit — a case declaring a departure with no reason, or a reason with no departure, exits 2. Prose in a header is not diffable; a column is. **THE SECOND DEPARTURE, found by building the fixture rather than by reading either side:** iOS's header documents one difference, that an unfound target stays nil instead of falling back to "pending". The real count is **6 of 18 cases**, because iOS also returns nil when the target IS loaded but carries no row, no stamp and no mirror and is not finished — where the web's fallback still ends in "pending". That shape was written down nowhere. All 6 have the same form, iOS declining to guess where the web guesses, and the Swift suite asserts that as a PROPERTY, so a departure of any other shape fails as a new rule instead of passing as an old one. **TO BUILD IT, THE WEB'S RULE HAD TO BECOME REAL CODE.** It was inline in the finish-request card, so a fixture would have restated it rather than computed from it; `requestStatusOf` now lives in `src/finishRequests.js` and the card calls it. On the way out its `status === "Finished"` became `isClosedStatus(...)` — the `statusLiteralViolations` ratchet caught the relocation, which is exactly what it is for — so the TRAQS.jsx baseline drops 69 → 68 and the comparison survives a spelling drift. **GATE PROVEN RATHER THAN ASSUMED:** mutating the web rule's fallback makes the suite fail and NAME the five cases that changed. The fixture also holds eight properties about itself so it cannot rot into a rubber stamp — among them that it contains both agreeing and departing cases, since a fixture of all agreement tests nothing and one of all departure is not parity. Wired into `npm run build` (106 suites). **THE SWIFT HALF IS WRITTEN AND NOT COMPILED**, per the standing rule: it rides with the next Mac build alongside #447, and what that build answers is whether `FinishParityTests` passes against the committed file — in particular whether `CompletionRequestRules.status` really returns nil in all 6 declared cases, which is asserted from READING the Swift, not from running it. **WHY NOW, WHILE THE TWO AGREE ON EVERY LIVE RECORD:** this rule is the one handshake that CROSSES the platforms — raised on iOS, approved on the web — so a divergence is guaranteed to reach a user, and today's agreement is what makes it cheap to pin rather than a reason to skip it.

479. [BUILT 2026-10-08, Trey's] **START, END AND EST. H ARE EDITABLE IN THE JOB DETAILS TABLE — AND HALF OF THOSE CELLS ARE DERIVED, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS NOT SIX IDENTICAL INPUTS.** One `jdCell(node, key, kind, parentId, own)` helper, committing through the SAME `commitCellEdit` the General card already used, so start and end inherit #398's routing for free: the `moveJobs` permission rather than `editJobs`, the refusal chain, the unscheduled-date hold in `schedDraft` (one save for date-plus-assignee) and the moveLog entry that `commitDates` writes. Nothing new had to be plumbed; the only change at the call site is passing the ROW's id and its parent instead of the job's. **THE PART THAT NEEDED A RULING FROM THE CODE RATHER THAN A PREFERENCE: AN EDITABLE CELL OVER A COMPUTED VALUE IS A DEFECT — you type, and the next roll-up puts it back.** `rollUpJobDates` recomputes a panel's start and end from its DATED ops, and `_panelHoursPair` SUMS the ops' estimates whenever there are any; only an op is a true leaf. So the panel cells test ownership first — `panelOwnsDates` and `panelOwnsHours`, each written as the negation of the exact condition in the code that would overwrite it, so the cell and the roll-up cannot drift apart — and a panel that owns neither keeps rendering the rolled-up number in its own styling. **MEASURED: only 7 of 123 panels (5.7%) own their own hours, and the same 7 own their own dates**, so for 94% of panels this correctly shows a total and offers no editor. All 395 ops are editable at every one of the three. **`scripts/jd-inline-cells-test.mjs`, red-first (14 failing), 32 assertions, 15 mutants, 15 caught, 0 survived.** THREE SURVIVED THE FIRST ROUND AND ALL THREE WERE MISSING ASSERTIONS, not bad mutants: nothing checked that an UNCHANGED value declines to write (every blur would otherwise be a save, and for start/end that is a moveLog entry recording a move that never happened), nothing checked that a negative estimate is clamped or that an emptied cell clears rather than writing 0, and — **R4 again, for the third time this week** — a single `commitCellEdit(node.id, …, parentId)` regex matched the NUMBER branch and stood in for the DATE branch, so a mutant dropping `parentId` from the date cell survived. One regex over two sites is not an assertion about either; both branches are now pinned separately.

480. [FOUND 2026-10-08 while building #479; LOGGED, NOT FIXED, on Trey's instruction] **A TYPED ESTIMATE WRITES NO MOVE LOG, WHILE THE SAME CHANGE BY DRAGGING DOES.** `commitCellEdit` names `start` and `end` as schedule fields and routes them to `commitDates`; `hpd` is not named, so it falls through to `updTask` with the `editJobs` permission and no placement work at all. Dragging a bar's edge takes the other path: `applyDragMove` with `hpd` on the mover sets `resized`, and `moveLogEntry` records `fromHpd`/`toHpd`. So **the same change to the same field is recoverable history through one gesture and invisible through the other** — and an estimate is not a cosmetic field: it is the denominator of every progress percentage on the board. **THIS IS PRE-EXISTING, NOT INTRODUCED BY #479.** The job-level `jdField("hpd", "Est. hours / day", "number")` has had exactly this property since it shipped; #479 extends the same behaviour to 395 ops and 7 panels rather than changing it, which is why it was logged rather than folded in — Trey's call, and the right one: a resize is a different gesture from an edit and routing `hpd` through `resizeShare` is a change with its own refusal consequences, not a side change. **NOT PROPOSED YET**, because the question underneath it is whether a typed estimate SHOULD be refusable at all: `commitDates` can refuse a date for overlap, PTO or a lock, and refusing an estimate for an overlap it has nothing to do with is the same mistake the `dueDate` carve-out already names in that function's own comment.

481. [MEASURED 2026-10-08] **THE SEVEN CAPABILITIES THE EDIT WIZARD HAS AND JOB DETAILS DOES NOT, counted on the live board, because whether each one moves, stays or goes turns on whether anybody uses it.** `scripts/measure-wizard-only.mjs`, across 65 jobs, 123 panels and 395 ops. **DEPENDENCIES: 8 of 395 ops (2.0%), 21 edges, confined to TWO jobs** — Brigham GCC #3 and Brush Creek — **and `depsMode` is UNSET on all 123 panels**, so the three-mode control the wizard offers has never been used once. **TEMPLATE LOAD: NOT MEASURABLE, and that is the finding rather than a gap in the method** — templates live in `localStorage` under `tq_templates_<org>`, per browser, so nothing in S3 records that one was ever loaded; only the person holding them can answer. **qty: 0 of 518 nodes.** Not one record carries a quantity at all; the field is absent everywhere. **requiredDepartments: 51 of 395 ops (12.9%)**, four departments in use (Layout 17, Cut 17, Wire 13, Admin 4), plus 3 of 123 panels — the most used of the seven by a wide margin, and the one with a real claim to belong on the page. **DRAG-TO-REORDER: at most 4 of 107 multi-op panels (3.7%), and this number is INFERRED AND WEAK** — it counts panels whose stored op order differs from their title order, which cannot tell an op created out of sequence from one deliberately dragged. It is an upper bound, not a count. **OVERRIDE START DATE: 4 of 65 jobs carry `isReschedule`**; `_rescheduleStartDate` reads 0 because #462 cleaned it and `stripDerived` now removes it on every save, so that zero says the field is no longer PERSISTED, not that the control is unused — a distinction worth making because the obvious reading is the wrong one. `scheduledLater` is on 7 jobs as a KEY and TRUE on none, the same key-versus-value trap that made `pendingFinish` read as 23 in #476. **PER-OP hpd: 392 of 395 ops (99.2%) carry an estimate**, which is the one capability that was unambiguously missing and is now inline. **WHAT THE NUMBERS SAY, as a reading rather than a decision:** one of the seven is heavily used (requiredDepartments), one was the real gap and is closed (per-op hpd), three are effectively unused (deps at 2% with the mode control at 0%, qty at 0%, reorder at ≤3.7%), one cannot be measured from here (templates), and one is a flag on 4 jobs whose storage was just removed (override start).

482. [FOUND 2026-10-08 in #481's measurement] **`scheduledLater` IS ON 7 JOBS AS A KEY AND TRUE ON NONE — THE THIRD TIME THIS EXACT TRAP HAS BEEN HIT IN A WEEK.** A scan that counts `"scheduledLater" in job` reports **7**; one that counts `job.scheduledLater === true` reports **0**. Both are correct about different questions, and only one of them is about the product. The same shape produced #476's `pendingFinish` (23 keys, 0 true, so a finish-request claim read as live when it was latent) and #466's first pass at `duplicateJob` (counting key presence made the carried-field list look larger than it is). **THE RULE THIS KEEPS TEACHING: A BOOLEAN FIELD HAS THREE STATES IN STORED JSON — absent, `false`, and `true` — and only the third is the feature being used.** `in` and truthiness answer different questions, and the measurement is almost always about the value. Where a count of keys IS what you want, it is for a different reason (how many records a writer has touched), and saying which one you are counting belongs in the output rather than in the reader's head. **WHAT `scheduledLater` ACTUALLY IS has not been established and is NOT claimed here** — it is written by something, it is false everywhere it exists, and no live job uses it. It is listed beside `isReschedule` (4 jobs, all genuinely true) in the wizard's own state, so the two are probably a pair; that is an inference, marked as one, and it is not worth resolving until the Edit-wizard question is settled, because the answer may delete the field with the control that writes it.

483. [FIXED 2026-10-08; A LIVE REGRESSION, THREE DAYS OLD] **EVERY DEFAULT-TRIGGER DROPDOWN IN THE APP SHOWED ITS PLACEHOLDER INSTEAD OF ITS VALUE.** Trey reported it as "clientId shows empty when a client is set" on the Job Details General card. It is not a card bug and it is not the client field: it is `SimpleDrop` itself, and it affects **9 of its 10 call sites**. `const rows = open ? listOf() : []` makes the option list empty while the menu is CLOSED — deliberately, so a thunk stays unresolved — and `const sel = rows.find(o => o.value === value)` read the selection out of that empty list. The trigger renders `{sel ? sel.label : placeholder}`, so a closed dropdown could only ever render the placeholder, and the real label appeared **only while the menu was open**. **DATED, NOT GUESSED: `99e2785`, 2026-10-05**, which replaced `const sel = options.find(o => o.value === value)` with the lazy form while adding a thunk and a custom trigger for the Jobs-page quick-assign. The laziness is correct — `assignPickerFor` is expensive — but it was applied to the one value that has to be known while closed. **THE FIX KEEPS THE LAZINESS EXACTLY WHERE IT WAS WANTED, and the measurement is what made that possible: the ONE caller with an expensive thunk is the SAME caller that passes a custom `trigger`**, which renders its own content and never reads `sel`. So `sel` is skipped entirely for a custom trigger and resolved from the full list otherwise — `trigger ? null : listOf().find(…)` — which costs nothing for the nine array callers and leaves the quick-assign thunk unresolved until the menu opens. **MEASURED ACROSS THE WHOLE CARD, NOT JUST THE REPORTED FIELD, as instructed:** the General card has **four** selects — `clientId`, `status`, `projectManagerId`, `pri` — and all four were showing "—". The other six fields are text, date, number and textarea, which never read `sel` and were never affected. The two id-bearing selects already stringify on BOTH sides (`value: String(c.id)` and `toValue: v => String(v)`), so the id drift that broke #384, #444 and #469 is not present here; `status` and `pri` are plain strings with no conversion, and `withCurrent` prepends the job's own value when it is off-list so an imported spelling still renders. **`scripts/simpledrop-selection-test.mjs`, red-first (2 failing), 21 assertions, 8 mutants, 8 caught, 0 survived.** THE SUITE EXECUTES SIMPLEDROP'S OWN THREE LINES rather than a restatement of them — `listOf`, `rows` and `sel` are cut out of the source and run against fixtures, so it fails if the source changes shape instead of passing against a copy that cannot drift (LESSONS #1), and it aborts rather than reporting if it can no longer find them (LESSONS #16). ONE MUTANT SURVIVED THE FIRST ROUND — loosening `===` to `==` — because every fixture used strings on both sides, so the two compares were indistinguishable. The case that separates them is a NUMERIC option value against a stored string, which is exactly the drift family; it is now asserted both ways, because the strict compare is what makes the call sites' `String()` the real fix rather than a decoration.
